=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given a string source of size n, a string pattern that is a subsequence of source, and a sorted integer array targetIndices that contains distinct numbers in the range [0, n - 1].
We define an operation as removing a character at an index idx from source such that:

idx is an element of targetIndices.
pattern remains a subsequence of source after removing the character.

Performing an operation does not change the indices of the other characters in source. For example, if you remove 'c' from "acb", the character at index 2 would still be 'b'.
Return the maximum number of operations that can be performed.
 
Example 1:

Input: source = "abbaa", pattern = "aba", targetIndices = [0,1,2]
Output: 1
Explanation:
We can't remove source[0] but we can do either of these two operations:

Remove source[1], so that source becomes "a_baa".
Remove source[2], so that source becomes "ab_aa".


Example 2:

Input: source = "bcda", pattern = "d", targetIndices = [0,3]
Output: 2
Explanation:
We can remove source[0] and source[3] in two operations.

Example 3:

Input: source = "dda", pattern = "dda", targetIndices = [0,1,2]
Output: 0
Explanation:
We can't remove any character from source.

Example 4:

Input: source = "yeyeykyded", pattern = "yeyyd", targetIndices = [0,2,3,4]
Output: 2
Explanation:
We can remove source[2] and source[3] in two operations.

 
Constraints:

1 <= n == source.length <= 3 * 10^3
1 <= pattern.length <= n
1 <= targetIndices.length <= n
targetIndices is sorted in ascending order.
The input is generated such that targetIndices contains distinct elements in the range [0, n - 1].
source and pattern consist only of lowercase English letters.
The input is generated such that pattern appears as a subsequence in source.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

DP implementation:

class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(source)
        m = len(pattern)
        is_target = [False] * n
        for idx in targetIndices:
            is_target[idx] = True

        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (m + 1)
        dp[0] = 0

        for i, ch in enumerate(source):
            cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
            # update backwards
            # local variables for speed
            # If cost 0 and dp[m] already 0, can return len(targetIndices) maybe after any point? Actually if dp[m]==0 at any prefix, final min 0. We can return immediately? Wait dp[m]==0 means there is a full pattern match using no target positions among processed prefix. Since future source chars are not needed; this matching remains valid in full source. So yes min_kept=0. Then answer len(targetIndices). We can return early. But careful: if dp[m] becomes 0, we can return len(targetIndices) immediately. But if we return before processing all source, no issue. However, if we break, we need not process. We can add:
            if dp[m] == 0:
                return len(targetIndices)
        But check before processing? At start maybe pattern empty? pattern length >=1, dp[m] not 0 unless m=0. So no. After each char, if dp[m]==0, return. But is it possible dp[m]==0 using processed prefix but some target positions before processed? It means no target positions used in that match. Then all target positions can be deleted, answer len(targetIndices). Yes. But wait if dp[m]==0 at prefix, the matching uses no target positions in prefix; future non-target/target positions irrelevant. We can delete all target positions, final remaining contains that matching. So answer len(targetIndices). Return. Good.
            for j in range(m, 0, -1):
                if ch == pattern[j-1]:
                    val = dp[j-1] + cost
                    if val < dp[j]:
                        dp[j] = val
            # optional early if dp[m]==0 after update
            if dp[m] == 0:
                return len(targetIndices)
        return len(targetIndices) - dp[m]

But wait: If dp[m]==0 before processing all source, returning len(targetIndices) is correct. But what if dp[m]==0 due to using a non-target position that is in targetIndices? cost 0 means not target. Good.

However, early return inside loop could be problematic if dp[m] is INF? no.

Need think about descending update with early return after each char. Suppose dp[j-1] was updated earlier in same iteration? Descending prevents. But early return after update might return before processing remaining source; no issue.

Could optimize by skipping inner loop if cost? Not needed.

source "a", pattern "a", target []? constraints targetIndices length >=1? It says 1 <= targetIndices.length <= n, so not empty. But if empty, ans 0. Not needed.

source "aa", pattern "a", target [0]. ans 1-1=0? Wait targetIndices length 1, source "aa", pattern "a", target [0]. We can remove index0? After removal source "a" (original index1) pattern remains, so max operations 1. DP: i0 a cost1 dp1=1; i1 a cost0 dp1=min(1,0)=0; early return lenT=1. Good. If target [1], i0 cost0 dp1=0 early return 1. Remove index1, source "a" index0 remains, pattern remains. Good.

for i, ch in enumerate(source):
    cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
    # If cost 0, update all matching j; if cost 1, same.
    # We can iterate only j where pattern[j-1] == ch. But checking all j is okay.
    for j in range(m, 0, -1):
        if pattern[j-1] == ch:
            nd = dp[j-1] + cost
            if nd < dp[j]:
                dp[j] = nd
    if dp[m] == 0:
        return len(targetIndices)

Pseudo:

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(source)
        m = len(pattern)
        is_target = [False] * n
        for idx in targetIndices:
            is_target[idx] = True

        positions = [[] for _ in range(26)]
        for j, ch in enumerate(pattern, 1):
            positions[ord(ch) - 97].append(j)

        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (m + 1)
        dp[0] = 0

        for i, ch in enumerate(source):
            cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
            for j in reversed(positions[ord(ch) - 97]):
                val = dp[j - 1] + cost
                if val < dp[j]:
                    dp[j] = val
            if dp[m] == 0:
                return len(targetIndices)

        return len(targetIndices) - dp[m]

The subsequence matching uses original indices; deleting earlier target positions doesn't remove later non-target. Good.

Implementation:

class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(source)
        m = len(pattern)
        is_target = [False] * n
        for idx in targetIndices:
            is_target[idx] = True

        pos = [[] for _ in range(26)]
        for idx, ch in enumerate(pattern, 1):
            pos[ord(ch) - 97].append(idx)

        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (m + 1)
        dp[0] = 0

        for i, ch in enumerate(source):
            cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
            lst = pos[ord(ch) - 97]
            for j in reversed(lst):
                cand = dp[j - 1] + cost
                if cand < dp[j]:
                    dp[j] = cand
            if dp[m] == 0:
                return len(targetIndices)

        return len(targetIndices) - dp[m]

Before final, let's re-evaluate the reduction with a formal proof to ensure no subtle "max operations" vs "max deletions" issue. Suppose we have a set D of target positions to delete. If final remaining contains pattern, then any sequence deleting all positions in D is valid? We said intermediate remaining is superset of final remaining. But wait, if we delete positions in D in some order, at an intermediate step, the remaining set is original minus a subset of D. This is indeed a superset of final remaining (original minus D). So if final remaining contains pattern, intermediate contains pattern. Thus all sequences valid. Therefore maximum operations equals maximum |D| such that original \ D contains pattern. Since D subset targetIndices. Equivalent minimize |targetIndices \ D| = number of target positions kept. The kept positions must include at least one subsequence matching pattern? Actually original \ D contains pattern iff there exists a matching of pattern entirely within original \ D. That matching may use target positions that are kept and non-target positions. It cannot use deleted target positions. So there exists a set K of target positions kept such that there is a matching using only non-target positions plus K. We can choose K as the target positions in that matching; any additional kept target positions are unnecessary. Thus min kept target positions = min over matchings of number of target positions in matching. Yes.

Now, one subtlety: If original \ D contains pattern, the matching may use some target positions that are in D? No, D deleted, so no. Thus K = targetIndices \ D must contain all target positions used by matching. So |K| >= min target count in matching. Conversely, if choose a matching with k target positions, let D = all targetIndices except those k positions. Then original \ D contains matching, so pattern remains. Thus max |D| = len(targetIndices) - min k. Good.

</think>

=== ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maxRemovals(self, source: str, pattern: str, targetIndices: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(source)
        m = len(pattern)

        is_target = [False] * n
        for idx in targetIndices:
            is_target[idx] = True

        pos = [[] for _ in range(26)]
        for j, ch in enumerate(pattern, 1):
            pos[ord(ch) - ord('a')].append(j)

        INF = 10**9
        dp = [INF] * (m + 1)
        dp[0] = 0

        for i, ch in enumerate(source):
            cost = 1 if is_target[i] else 0
            for j in reversed(pos[ord(ch) - ord('a')]):
                cand = dp[j - 1] + cost
                if cand < dp[j]:
                    dp[j] = cand

            if dp[m] == 0:
                return len(targetIndices)

        return len(targetIndices) - dp[m]
```
